Proposition 5.4.1
.

Functors
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}
and
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}

have the following properties

•

The functor
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is a right adjoint of
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}
,

•

The functor
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is monoidal.

•

The diagram (
5.3
) is commutative.

Proof.

The first and second statements are shown by repeating the argument from the proof
of
5.2.1
. The third statement follow from the base change since
an open embedding is a smooth map. In more details, let us argue for
statement about
\mathrm{CH}^{\bullet}
. Indeed, for an element
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fr}}
we have

j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{C})=j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}*}(\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{CH}}\otimes(j^{0}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{C})))^{T})=\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}*}\Bigg(j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\bigg(\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{CH}}\otimes(j^{0}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{C})))^{T}\bigg)\Bigg).

Next we observe that stability condition on the critical locus of the potential on
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\mathrm{CH}}
can be imposed
requiring stability of the image of the push-forward along
\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}}
.
Hence, the map
j_{\mathrm{fs}}
preserves the critical part of fibers of the projection

\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}}
. On the other hand the functor

\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}(-)^{T}
is constant along the fibers of
\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}}
. Thus last expression is equal:

\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}*}\Bigg(\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg(j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\bigg(\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{CH}}\otimes(j^{0}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{C})\bigg)\bigg)^{T}\Bigg)=\pi_{\mathrm{Dr}*}\Bigg(\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}\bigg(j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{CH}})\otimes(j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}\circ j^{0}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{C})\bigg)^{T}\Bigg).

Finally, we use the base change we get

j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ j_{*}^{0}\circ f_{\Delta}^{*}=j^{0}_{*}\circ j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}=j^{0}_{*}\circ f^{*}_{\Delta}\circ j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}.

Combining with the previous computation we obtain

j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}\circ j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{C}).

Similar, argument implies

j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{D})=\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}\circ j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{D}),\quad\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fr}}.

∎

5.5.
Linearized categories

In the case of
\bullet=\mathrm{fs}
we show that the linearized category is actually equivalent to the
original category, thus we complete the construction of the functor

\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
introduced at the beginning of the section.

The variety
\mathscr{C}=\mathfrak{g}\times G\times\mathfrak{g}
has coordinates
(Z,g,X)
.
A coordinate substitution
Z=Rg
,
R\in\mathfrak{g}

on our main variety
\mathscr{C}
makes the potential tri-linear:

\underline{W}_{\mathrm{Dr}}(R,g,X)=\textup{Tr}(X[g,R])=W_{\mathrm{Dr}}(Rg,g,X).

The framed potential also linearizes:

\underline{W}_{\mathrm{Dr}}^{\mathrm{fr}}(R,g,X,v,w)=\underline{W}_{\mathrm{Dr}}(R,g,X)+\textup{Tr}(w(v-gv)).

Thus we introduce linearized categories:

\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}:=\mathrm{MF}_{G}(\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\bullet},\underline{W}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{lin}}),

where
\mathscr{C}^{\bullet}
is the open set in
\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\bullet}
(the group
G=\textup{GL}_{n}

an affine open subset of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
)
and
\underline{W}^{\mathrm{fs}}=\underline{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}
. That is

\underline{\mathscr{C}}=\mathfrak{g}^{3},\quad\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\mathrm{fr}}=\mathfrak{g}^{3}\times V_{G}\times V_{G}^{*}.

Since
j_{G}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\bullet}
is an open embedding, the pull-back functor
j_{G}^{*}

is a localization functor and we denote

\mathrm{loc}^{\bullet}\colon\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}\rightarrow\mathrm{MF}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}

for this functor.

Remark 5.5.1
.

The categories
\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
do not have a monoidal structure that respects the localization functor.

Proposition 5.5.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

The compliment to
j_{G}(\mathscr{C})^{\mathrm{fs}}
inside
\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is defined
by the equation
\det(g)=0
.
Then according to the lemma 12.3 from
[
OR18c
]
,
the pull back to a Zarisky open subset is an equivalence of the categories
of matrix factorizations as long as the open set contains all critical points
of the potential.
Thus it is sufficient to show that the zero locus of
\det(g)=0
does not intersect the critical locus of the potential.

The critical locus
Z_{crit}
is given by the system of equations:

[X,R]-wv=0,\quad[g,X]=[g,R]=0,\quad wg=w,\quad gv=v.

These equations appear in the description of the moduli space
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}}
is the
section
5.7
. Moreover, the stability condition for

\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is equivalent to the stability condition for
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}
. In other words

Z_{crit}\subset\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}\times\mathfrak{g}.

On other hand proposition
5.7.1
and the last three equations for
Z_{crit}
imply that
g=Id
.
Slightly rephrasing, we can argue that the stability condition in section
5.3
says that
v
is a
cyclic vector for
X
and
Z=Rg.
If g commutes with
X
and
R
then it
also commutes with
Z
, so
gv=v
implies that
g=Id.

∎

Remark 5.5.3
.

The stability condition of
\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}
transported by
\mathrm{CH}^{fs}
defines a point

(Z,g,X,v,w)\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{fr}}=\mathfrak{g}\times G\times\mathfrak{g}\times V_{G}\times V_{G}^{*}

to be stable if
\mathbb{C}\langle Z,X\rangle v=V_{G}
. On the other hand by the critical locus argument of the previous proposition the last open condition is equivalent
to a condition
\mathbb{C}\langle R,X\rangle v=V_{G}
.

5.6.
Koszul duality

Recall a general setup of the Koszul duality. We are interested in the version which provides a matrix factorization model for the differential graded category
of the derived complete intersection. The Koszul duality in this context was discussed in
[
AK15
]
where the results
of
[
Orl11
]
and
[
Isi13
]
are combined. In this note we present slightly more streamlined
construction of the duality functor.

Derived algebraic geometry is explained in many places, here we present a derived algebraic geometry treatment of derived complete intersections
in the most elementary setting sufficient for our needs.

Initial data for an affine derived complete intersection is a collection of elements

f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]
. In this section we assume that
X
is smooth, affine and of finite dimension. It determines the differential graded algebra

\mathcal{R}=(\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{*}U,D),\quad D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}},

where
\theta_{i}
from a basis of
U=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and the variables
\theta_{i}
anti-commute
\theta_{i}\theta_{j}=-\theta_{j}\theta_{i}
.
Thus algebra
\mathcal{R}
is super-commutative.

Respectively, we define dg category of coherent sheaves on

\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R})
as

\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R}))=\frac{\{\text{bounded complexes of finitely generated $\mathcal{R}$ dg modules}\}}{\{\text{quasi-isomorphisms}\}}.

We forfeit the homological grading in our setting and we assume that the complexes
are two periodic. In our proofs we use usual complexes to simplify
notations. Two periodic folding turns usual complexes to two-periodic:

(\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{i},D_{i}:M_{i}\to M_{i+1})\Longrightarrow(\mathbb{M}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathbb{D}_{i}:\mathbb{M}_{i}\to\mathbb{M}_{i+1}),

\mathbb{M}_{0}=\oplus_{i\in 2\mathbb{Z}}M_{i},\quad\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus_{i\in 1+2\mathbb{Z}}M_{i},\quad\mathbb{D}_{0}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}D_{2i},\quad\mathbb{D}_{0}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}D_{2i+1}.

Thus defined dg category generalizes the derived category of two-periodic complexes

\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathbf{Z})
, in the following sense. If the intersection
\mathbf{Z}

of
f_{i}=0
,
i=1,\dots,m
is transverse and
\mathbf{Z}
is smooth then ( by applying two-periodic folding to

[
Ras14
]
):

\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R}))=\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathbf{Z}).

Suppose that the ambient space
\mathbf{X}
carries an action of a group
G
and
that the ideal
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
is preserved by the action:

g(f_{i})=\sum_{j}a(g)_{ij}f_{j}
,
a_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}[X]
,
g\in G
. The matrix
(a(g)^{-1})^{*}
defines an action of
g\in G
on
U^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]
and the corresponding
G
-action preserves the differential
D
. In this setting
we have a well-defined
G
-equivariant dg category

\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R}),G)
.

Consider a potential on
\mathbf{X}\times U
:

(5.4)

W=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x)z_{i},

where
z_{i}
is a basis of
U^{*}
dual to the basis
\theta_{i}
. For the Koszul matrix factorization:

\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,W)\ni\mathbb{B}=(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbb{C}[U],D_{\mathbb{B}}),\quad D_{\mathbb{B}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\theta_{i}+f_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}.

We have
D_{\mathbb{B}}^{2}=W
because of (
5.4
).
For a
(M,D_{M})
dg module over
\mathcal{R}
, the tensor product

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}(M):=M\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[X]\otimes\Lambda^{*}(U)}\mathbb{B}

is an object of
\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,W)
with the differential
D=D_{M}\otimes 1+1\otimes D_{\mathbb{B}}
. In the last formula we use usual tensor product, not the derived one,
because
\mathbb{B}
is free over
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{*}(U)
.
The map
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}
extends to a functor between triangulated categories:

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}\colon\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R}))\rightarrow\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,W).

The functor in the other direction is based on the dual matrix factorization:

\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,-W)\ni B^{*}=(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathbb{C}[U],D_{B}^{*}),\quad D^{*}_{\mathbb{B}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\theta_{i}+f_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}},

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}\colon\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,W)\rightarrow\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R})),\quad\mathrm{KSZ}^{*}_{U}(\mathcal{F}):=\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}\times U]}\mathrm{B}^{*},\mathcal{R}).

Theorem 5.6.1
.

Suppose
X
is smooth and quasi-affine.
Then the functors
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}
and
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}
are mutually inverse:

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}\simeq\mathbf{D},\quad\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}\simeq\mathbf{D},

where
\mathbf{D}
are respective duality functors. In particular, for a dg
\mathcal{R}
-module
M
we define
\mathbf{D}(M)=\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(M^{\bullet},\mathcal{R})
where

M^{\bullet}
is a free resolution.

Proof.

Let us indicate the main observation behind the argument.
To simplify notations we assume that

m=1
and
\mathbf{X}
is affine. Respectively, we set
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}][z]
to be a ring of regular functions on

\mathbf{X}\times\mathbb{C}_{z}
and the underlying super-commutative ring of
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}][\theta]
.
First we prove that
\mathrm{KSZ}^{*}_{U}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}=\mathbf{D}
.
A dg
\mathcal{R}
-module
M

has a free resolution
M^{\bullet}
since

X
is an affine space. Thus the composition
\mathrm{KSZ}^{*}_{U}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}(M)
is
equal to

(5.5)

\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(B^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}][z]}B,\mathcal{R})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\check{M}^{\bullet}=(\textup{Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}][z,\theta^{(1)},\theta^{(2)}],\mathcal{R}),\check{D}_{\mathbb{BB}})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\check{M}^{\bullet},

where
\check{M}^{\bullet}=\mathbf{D}(M^{\bullet})
and the differential
\check{D}_{\mathbb{BB}}
is dual to the differential

D_{\mathbb{BB}}=z\theta_{-}+f(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{+}},\quad\theta_{\pm}=\theta^{(1)}\pm\theta^{(2)}

acting on
\mathbb{B}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[X][z]}\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{C}[X][z,\theta^{(1)},\theta^{(2)}]
.

Since
\theta_{-}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{+}}
commute, the differential
D_{\mathbb{BB}}
is a sum of two differentials:
D_{\mathbb{BB}}^{(1)}=z\theta_{-}
and
D_{\mathbb{BB}}^{(2)}=f(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{+}}
. Next we analyze the complex for the differentials that is dual to

D_{\mathbb{BB}}^{(1)}
.
Indeed, there is a homotopy equivalence of the
complexes of
\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]
-modules

(5.6)

(\mathbb{C}[z,\theta_{-}],z\theta_{-})\sim[\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]\xrightarrow{\theta_{-}}\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]\xrightarrow{\theta_{-}}\mathbb{C}[\theta]\xrightarrow{\theta_{-}}\dots].

The duality functor
\textup{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]}(\bullet,\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}])
inverts the arrow, and we obtain a resolution of
the simple
\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]
-module:

(5.7)

[\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]\xleftarrow{\theta_{-}}\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]\xleftarrow{\theta_{-}}\mathbb{C}[\theta_{-}]\xleftarrow{\theta_{-}}\dots]\sim\mathbb{C}.

Hence the complex (
5.5
) is quasi-isomorphic to
\check{M}^{\bullet}
and the statement follows.

The analysis of the composition in other direction
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}^{*}_{U}
is even easier, because it is reduced to the
study of the dg module

(5.8)

(\mathbb{B}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[X][\theta]}\mathbb{B}^{*},D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{BB}}),\quad D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{BB}}=\theta(z^{(1)}+z^{(2)}),

which is quasi-isomorphic to the dg module
\mathbb{C}[X][z^{(1)},z^{(2)}]/(z^{(1)}+z^{(2)})
with the trivial differential. Hence the statement follows.

The action on the
of morphisms between the objects is defined in a standard way:

(5.9)

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}(\phi)=\phi\otimes 1,\quad\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}(\Psi)=\Psi^{\vee}\otimes 1,

where
\Psi^{\vee}\in\textup{Hom}(\mathcal{G}^{\vee},\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
is dual to
\Psi\in\textup{Hom}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
.

The homotopy relation (
5.7
) only affects the second tensor factor in the
LHS of the formulas (
5.9
).
Thus the construction applied to the space of morphism
yields

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}(\Psi)=\Psi^{\vee},\quad\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}(\phi)=\phi^{\vee}.

Thus
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}
and
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}^{*}
are fully faithful functors that is bijective on isomorphism classes of objects thus these functors are equivalences.

So far we discussed the case
m=1
and
X
affine. In the case
m>1
we need work with the tensor product of complexes
K_{i}=(\mathbb{C}[z_{i},\theta_{i,-}],z_{i}\theta_{i,-})
,
i=1,\dots,m
,

\theta_{i,-}=\theta_{i}^{(1)}-\theta_{i}^{(1)}
instead of one complex (
5.6
). For each
K_{i}
we have the contraction (
5.7
) of the dual complex

K_{i}^{\vee}
and the rest of argument goes through. Similarly, for
m>1
we need to modify (
5.8
) by working with
D^{\prime}_{BB}=\sum_{i}\theta_{i}(z_{i}^{(1)}+z_{i}^{(2)})
.

The case of
\mathbf{X}
being is quasi-affine is completely parallel to the affine case since we can use apply the affine case proof to each term of the Cech resolution, see
section
2.1.5
.
∎

If we have a group
G
acting on
\mathbf{X}
and acting linearly on
U
and on
W\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}\times U]^{G}_{\chi}
,
\chi
is a character of
G
then the above functors extend to the isomorphism between the equivariant
version of the categories:

(5.10)

\textup{Coh}(\textup{Spec}(\mathcal{R}),G,\chi)\simeq\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,G,\chi,W).

In the last formula we work with the strongly equivariant matrix factorizations, thus all differentials and homotopies in the (curved) complexes are
G
-equivariant.
Hence no technical difficulties related to Chevalley-Eilenberg correction differential arise and argument from the above proves (
5.10
) after a slight adjustment of notations. Let us also point out that below we only need the Koszul duality for the strongly equivariant matrix factorizations since
the elements of
\mathrm{MF}_{\mathrm{Dr}}^{\bullet}
are strongly equivariant with respect to
G=\textup{GL}(n)
according to our main assumption (
4.5
).

Remark 5.6.2
.

Suppose that the scheme
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathbf{X}
defined by
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
is a smooth complete intersection.
Then
the functor
\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}
has a description in terms of push-forward functor. In more details,

\mathrm{KSZ}_{U}=i_{f=0,*}\circ\pi_{U}^{*}:D^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathbf{Z})=\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{Z},0)\to\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}\times U,W)

where
i_{f}
and

\pi_{U}
are natural inclusion and projection

i_{f=0}:\mathbf{Z}\times U\to\mathbf{X}\times U,\quad\pi_{U}:\mathbf{Z}\times U\to\mathbf{Z}.

5.7.
Koszul functors

In our setting
U=\mathfrak{g}
, while
\mathbf{Z}^{\bullet}=\mathscr{M}^{\bullet}\subset\mathbf{X}^{\bullet}
is a version of the commuting variety:

(5.11)

\mathscr{M}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{2}=\mathbf{X},\quad\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V_{G}\times V^{*}_{G}=\mathbf{X}^{\mathrm{fr}}.

The closed conditions describing the spaces
\mathscr{M}
are

(5.12)

[Z,X]=0,\quad Z,X\in\mathfrak{g},

for the spaces
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}
are

(5.13)

[Z,X]-vw=0,\quad Z,X\in\mathfrak{g},\quad v\in V_{G},w\in V^{*}_{G},

the open condition for the stable spaces is:

\mathbb{C}[Z,X]v=V_{G}.

Let us also recall a construction of
\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}
. The variety
\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}

is
G
a quotient of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V_{G}
where

(Z,X,v)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{n}\mbox{ iff }\mathbb{C}[Z,X]v=V_{G}.

The relation of
\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}
with moduli space
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is explained
in the book by Nakajima
[
Nak99
]
. First we observe that there is
a natural
G
-equivariant embedding

\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}\to\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}},\quad(Z,X,v)\mapsto(Z,X,v,0).

with
G
-action on
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}}
,

g\cdot(Z,X,v,w)=(\textup{Ad}_{g}Z,\textup{Ad}_{g}X,gv,wg^{-1}).

Proposition 5.7.1
.

[
Nak99
, Proposition 2.8, Theorem 1.9]
For any
n
we have

(1)

\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}=\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{n}
as subvariety of
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fr}}
.

(2)

For any
(Z,X,v)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{n}
its stabilizer is
\mathrm{Id}\in G
.

We define the dg algebra
\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}
as the algebra
\mathbb{C}[X^{\bullet}]\otimes\Lambda^{*}\mathfrak{g}
with the differential

(5.14)

D^{\bullet}=\sum_{ij}f_{ij}^{\bullet}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{ij}},

where
\theta_{ij}
is a basis of matrix units in
\mathfrak{g}
and
f^{\bullet}_{ij}
is the
ij
-entry of the corresponding
version of the defining equations (
5.12
), (
5.13
) for the embedding (
5.11
).

To simplify our notations we denote

\textup{Coh}^{\bullet}:=\textup{Coh}_{G}(\textup{Spec}{\mathcal{R}^{\bullet}}).

Finally, let us notice that in the case
\bullet=\mathrm{fs}
by proposition
5.7.1
the equations
f^{\mathrm{fs}}_{ij}
define a smooth complete intersection.
Hence, we have an equivalence of categories

(5.15)

\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{Dr}}\simeq\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{Dr}}\simeq\textup{Coh}^{\mathrm{fs}}\simeq\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}},q^{0}t).

Indeed, since the potential
\underline{W}^{\bullet}
is linear as a function of
g\in\mathfrak{g}
and the scaling torus
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
does not act on

g
, we obtain a pair of mutually inverse functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Coh}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}}

The tensor product of dg modules gives categories
\textup{Coh}^{\bullet}
monoidal structure. It appears that there is no natural monoidal
structure on the linearized category
\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
. Thus, it is not meaningful to discuss monomial structure of
the functor
\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. However, in the case
\bullet=\mathrm{fs}
we can use a sequence of equivalences to define a bivariant operation structure

(5.16)

\star:\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}},q^{0}t)\times\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}},q^{0}t)\to\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}},q^{0}t)

by intertwining the monoidal structure
\star
on
\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
, , see definition
5.1.1
, by the isomorphism (
5.15
).
Moreover, the composition
\textup{loc}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, that is used in (
5.15
), has the following monoidal properties. Below we use that the categories
\mathrm{MF}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
have monoidal structure
\star
, as described in definition
5.1.1
.

Proposition 5.7.2
.

Let
\tilde{M^{\prime}},\tilde{M^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathrm{MF}(\mathbf{X}^{\bullet},G,q^{0}t,0)
,
\bullet=\emptyset,\mathrm{fr},\mathrm{fs}
as in (
5.11
).
Respectively,
M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Coh}^{\bullet}
are corresponding complexes of
dg-modules.
Then there is an isomorphism

\textup{loc}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M^{\prime}\otimes M^{\prime\prime})\simeq\textup{loc}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M^{\prime})\star\textup{loc}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M^{\prime\prime})

Proof.

In the proof we examine the case
\bullet=\emptyset
, the other case are analogous.
The Koszul matrix factorization that is used in our construction of the Koszul duality
\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}
is

\mathbb{B}=[g-1,[X,R]]
, in notations of section
5.6
. On the other hand we have shown in proposition
4.5.8
that
\mathbb{B}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
(see (
4.21
)) is the
unit in
\mathrm{MF}_{\mathrm{Dr}}
.

Let us use notation from the definition
4.5.2
. In particular, the the coordinates on the convolution space
\mathfrak{g}\times G^{2}\times\mathfrak{g}
are
(Z,g_{1},g_{2},X)
. Respectively,
the convolution of
\mathbb{B}\star\mathbb{B}
is computed as push-forward along
\pi_{3}
of Koszul matrix factorization:

\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{B})=K(g_{1}-1)\otimes\pi_{3}^{*}(\mathbb{B}).

The last equality a consequence of the row transformation of the Koszul matrix factorizations and
K(g_{1}-1)
is the Koszul complex
of
g_{1}-1
. In more details:

\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{B})=[g_{1}-1,[X,Zg_{1}^{-1}]]\otimes[g_{2}-1,[X,g_{1}Zg_{1}^{-1}g_{2}^{-1}]]\\
=[g_{1}-1,[X,Zg_{1}^{-1}]]\otimes[g_{3}g_{1}^{-1}-1,[X,g_{1}Zg_{3}^{-1}]],

the last equation we used variable
g_{3}=g_{2}g_{1}
. Using row operations, see for example
[
OR18c
, section 2.3]
we eliminate all instances of
g_{1}
and
g_{1}^{-1}
from the second Koszul matrix factorization with help of the first matrix factorization. The result is

[g_{1}-1,R_{1}]\otimes[g_{3}-1,R_{2}]

where
R_{2}
does not depend on
g_{1}
.
The last matrix factorization has potential
\pi^{*}_{3}(W_{\mathrm{Dr}})
and this potential does not depend on
g_{1}
hence
R_{1}=0
. On the other hand
[g_{3}-1,R_{2}]
is the matrix factorization with the potential
\pi^{*}_{3}(W_{\mathrm{Dr}})
. Thus
R_{2}=[X,Zg_{3}^{-1}]
by the uniqueness lemma
[
OR18c
, Lemma 3.6]
since the entries of
g_{3}-1

form a regular sequence.

On the other hand

\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M^{\prime})\star\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M^{\prime\prime})
is a push-forward
\pi_{3*}
applied to

\pi_{1}^{*}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}}\mathbb{B})\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}(M^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}}\mathbb{B}).

By the previous remark we can rewrite the last expression as

\tilde{\pi}_{1}^{*}(\tilde{M^{\prime}})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\pi^{*}_{3}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{2}^{*}(\tilde{M^{\prime\prime}})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}K(g_{1}-1).

where
\mathcal{A}=\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g})=\pi_{2}^{*}(\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g})
and
\tilde{\pi}_{i}:\mathfrak{g}\times G^{2}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{2}
is a composition of
\pi_{i}
with a projection
along the group factor

p_{G}:\mathfrak{g}\times G\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{2}
.

Let us define map
\tilde{\pi}_{3}
similarly
\tilde{\pi}_{3}=p_{G}\circ\pi_{3}
.
The maps
\tilde{\pi}_{1}
,
\tilde{\pi}_{2}
coincide with the map
\tilde{\pi}_{3}

on the locus defined by
Z=\textup{Ad}_{g_{1}}Z
.
Thus the presence of the Koszul complex

K(g_{1}-1)
allows us to construct a homotopy

\tilde{\pi}_{2}^{*}(\tilde{M}^{\prime\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}K(g_{1}-1)\sim\tilde{\pi}_{3}^{*}(\tilde{M}^{\prime\prime})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}K(g_{1}-1),

Hence we arrive to the matrix factorization

\pi_{3}^{*}(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}}M^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{X}]\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}}\mathbb{B})\otimes K(g_{1}-1).

Applying
\pi_{3*}
to the last expression we obtain the statement by the projection
formula.
∎

Remark 5.7.3
.

It is predicted by Kapustin and Rozansky
[
KR10
]
, the monoidal structure on
\textup{Coh}^{\bullet}
induced by the Koszul duality, as in above proposition, is a deformation of the standard monoidal structure. We do not explore this fascinating structure in this paper.

5.8.
Localized Chern and co-Chern functors

Since
\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}}
is invertible, we can
use the Koszul duality functor in order to construct the functor

\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}\circ(\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}})^{-1}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}^{*}_{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2}),\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t).

The localization functor does not seem to be invertible in cases of
\bullet=\emptyset,\mathrm{fr}
however a construction of the
functor in the opposite direction does not require invertibility of the localization:

\mathrm{HC}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\mathrm{HC}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{loc}^{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\textup{Coh}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathrm{MF}^{\bullet},\quad\bullet=\emptyset,\mathrm{fr},\mathrm{fs}.

To complete the construction of functors
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, that is discussed in the introduction we need the following

Proposition 5.8.1
.

The functor
i_{\mathrm{fr}}
restricts to an isomorphism
i_{\mathrm{fs}}
between the stable
framed and stable categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{fs}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{fr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathrm{st}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}}

Proof.

The functors
i_{\mathrm{fr}}
and
i_{\mathrm{fs}}
are defined as composition
i_{w=0*}\circ\pi_{V}^{*}
. Both maps have presentations
i_{w=0}=\tilde{i}_{w=0}\times Id_{\mathcal{X}^{v}}
,
\pi_{V}=\tilde{\pi}\times Id_{\mathcal{X}_{v}}
. On the other hand the stability condition on
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{fr}}
is defined in terms of the
projection
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{fs}}\to\mathcal{X}^{v}
. Thus the maps in the diagram in the statement form a commutative diagram. Finally, the upper arrow is an isomorphism
because
\tilde{i}_{w=0*}\circ\tilde{\pi}_{V}^{*}
is the Knorrer equivalence of categories
3.6.1
.
∎

Thus we can define the functors
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}
by the commutative diagram:

(5.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{Dr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\mathrm{Dr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{fs}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{fs}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}}

As shown in
[
OR18b
]
, that there are monoidal functors from the finite braid group

\Phi\colon\mathfrak{Br}_{n}\to\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}}.

Since
\mathscr{X}^{v}=\mathscr{X}^{v}\times V_{G}
and the extra factor
V_{G}
does not participate in the convolution product (
5.1
) we have commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Br}_{n}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Br}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Br}_{n}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathrm{fr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathrm{aff}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathrm{aff}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\mathrm{aff}}}

In all cases the generators of the braid group are mapped to
Koszul matrix factorizations, hence the previous argument implies the following:

Proposition 5.8.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
is monoidal. Moreover,
its image is central:
for any
\mathcal{D}\in\mathrm{D}^{per}(\mathrm{Hilb})
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}

\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{D})\star\Phi(\beta)\sim\Phi(\beta)\star\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{D}).

Proof.

Let’s pick an affine lift
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathfrak{Br}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{n}
of
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
. Now we can use proposition (
5.3
) and proposition
5.8.1
:

\Phi(\beta^{-1})\star\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{D})\star\Phi(\beta)=\Phi(\beta^{-1})\star\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\star\Phi(\beta)\\
=j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}(\Phi^{\mathrm{aff}}(\tilde{\beta}^{-1})\star\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})\star\Phi^{\mathrm{aff}}(\tilde{\beta}))=j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}(\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}))=\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{D}),

here
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}

is such that
j_{\mathrm{fs}}^{*}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
∎

The last proposition and the previous diagram imply

Corollary 5.8.3
.

Functors
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}
and
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}

have the following properties

•

the functor
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
is a right adjoint of
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,

•

the functor
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
is monoidal,

•

the image of
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
commutes with the elements
\Phi(\beta)
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
.

6.
Knot invariants

In this section we discuss the relation between the Chern character and the functor of the braid closure that was used in our previous papers
[
OR18c
]
. There we constructed a link invariant
\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta)
of the closure of a braid

\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
. Our construction of the invariant is based on the homomorphism:

\mathfrak{Br}_{n}\to\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}},\quad\beta\mapsto\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta},

and the braid closure procedure

\mathbb{L}\colon\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}}\to\mathrm{MF}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}_{y=0}^{\mathrm{free}},B\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t,0),\quad\mathcal{C}\mapsto j^{*}_{e,G}(\mathcal{C})^{G},

where
\mathrm{MF}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}_{y=0}^{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t,0)
is the category of coherent sheaves on
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
, where

\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}=\{(X,Y,v)\in\mathfrak{b}\times\mathfrak{n}\times V_{B}\;|\;\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle\,v=V_{B}\},

where subindex
y=0
is chosen to indicate that the matrix
Y
is strictly upper-trianglular.

The space
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}\times G
embeds inside
\mathscr{X}
by a
G\times B^{2}
-equivariant map
j_{e,G}
used in the
construction of
\mathbb{L}
:

(6.1)

j_{e,G}\colon\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}\times G\rightarrow\mathscr{X},\quad(X,Y,g)\mapsto(X,g,Y,g,Y).

It is shown in
[
OR18c
]
that the triply-graded space

(6.2)

\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta):=\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbb{L}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}V)^{T},

is an isotopy invariant of the closure
L(\beta)
of the braid
\beta
(after an explicit
grading shift that is expressed in terms of writhe of the braid).

The quotient of the space
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
by
B
is the free flag Hilbert space
\mathrm{FHilb}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
.
This space is smooth, an explicit smooth atlas is presented in
[
OR18c
]
. The condition
of commutativity
[X,Y]=0

defines an embedding of the flag Hilbert scheme

\mathrm{FHilb}_{y=0}
inside
\mathrm{FHilb}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
.

The vector space
V_{B}
is a fiber of a trivial
B
-equivariant vector bundle on
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
which we denote the same symbol.
The vector bundle
V_{B}
becomes a vector bundle on the
B
-quotient
\mathrm{FHilb}^{\mathrm{free}}_{y=0}
, we use notation
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
for this vector bundle and

\mathcal{B}
for the dual bundle. Respectively, the trivial
G
-equivariant bundle with a fiber
V_{G}
over
\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{fs}}
descends to the vector
bundle on
\mathrm{Hilb}
which we denote by
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
and its dual by
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
.

Given a finite-dimensional representation
W_{G}
of
G
we denote by
W_{B}
the restriction of this representation to
B
. Given
B^{2}
-equivariant matrix
factorization
\mathcal{F}=(M,D,\partial)
we denote define the tensor product with
W_{B}
by

\mathcal{F}\otimes W_{B^{(1)}}=(M\otimes W_{B^{(1)}},D\otimes 1,\partial\otimes 1).

Similarly, we define the tensor product
\mathcal{G}\otimes W_{G}
for a
G
-equivariant matrix factorization.

The following is a simple consequence of the definition of the Chern functor:

Proposition 6.0.1
.

For any
m
the functor
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
intertwines the tensor powers of
V^{*}
and
\mathcal{B}
:

\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}\otimes(V^{*}_{B^{(1)}})^{\otimes m})=\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{\otimes m},\quad\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}

and this functor intertwines the action of the symmetric group
S_{m}
. The analogous statement holds for the
dual bundles:
V
and
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
.

As corollary we obtain the second statement of theorem
1.0.2
:

Corollary 6.0.2
.

For any
k
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
we have

\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\beta\cdot FT^{k}))=\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\beta))\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{k}

where
FT
is the full-twist braid.

Proof.

We show in
[
OR18b
]
that
\Phi(\beta\cdot FT^{k})=\Phi(\beta)\otimes(\Lambda^{n}V^{*}_{B^{(1)}})^{k}
. Hence the previous proposition implies the statement.
∎

Also the analog of the proposition
4.5.8
holds in the stable category:

Proposition 6.0.3
.

The element
\mathcal{O}\in\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t)
is the convolution unit and

\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{st}}.

Proof.

Let us define the units
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fr}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fs}}
in the categories

\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fr}}
,
\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{fs}}
by the formula analogous to (
4.21
):

\mathcal{O}^{\bullet}=[g-1,[X,Z]+wv]\in\mathrm{MF}_{\mathrm{Dr}}^{\bullet}.

The argument from the proof of proposition
4.5.8
can repeated word by
word in the framed situation to obtain:

\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fr}})=\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\parallel}.

Next we observe that
j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{fr}})=\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{fs}}

hence we combine this observation with proposition (
5.3
):

(6.3)

\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{fs}}=j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fr}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fr}}))=\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}(j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fr}}))=\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fs}}).

The equivalence
i_{\mathrm{fs}}
sends the unit
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{fs}}_{\parallel}
to the unit

\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\textup{st}}
. Thus we have:

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\parallel}=i_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{fs}})=i_{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fs}}).

Thus according to the diagram (
5.17
) we only need to check
that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{fs}}=\mathrm{loc}^{\mathrm{fs}}\circ\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{O})=j^{*}_{\mathrm{fs}}(\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{O}))
. Finally, matrix factorization
\mathrm{B}
the construction of the Koszul functor
\mathrm{KSZ}_{\mathfrak{g}}
in
theorem
5.6.1
is equal to the matrix factorization (
6.3
).
∎

Thus we can interpret the homology
\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta)
geometrically in terms of sheaves on the flag Hilbert schemes, see
[
OR18c
]
for a detailed discussion.
One unpleasant aspect of the flag Hilbert scheme is that the flag Hilbert scheme
\mathrm{FHilb}
is very singular and it is
hard to use the standard tools like localization for computations on it. Below we show how one can fix this issue
with the Chern functor.

Theorem 6.0.4
.

For any
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
there is an isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta)\cong\mathcal{E}\mathrm{xt}(\mathcal{O},\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}_{\beta}^{\mathrm{st}})\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B}).

Proof.

First, observe an isomorphism of complexes of

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}
-modules:

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{xt}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\parallel},\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta}\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}V^{*})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{st}}=j^{*}_{\mathrm{st}}\circ\pi_{V}^{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\parallel})
is the unit
in
\mathrm{MF}^{\mathrm{st}}
.
Indeed, the matrix factorization
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\parallel}
is
the Koszul matrix factorization for the defining ideal of the
B
-orbit of the image
j_{e,G}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}^{\mathrm{free}})
, and
since the action of
B
on the orbit is free, the statement follows (a more detailed argument can be found in the section 13 of
[
OR18c
]
).

Finally, we use the adjointness of the functors
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
:

\mathcal{E}\mathrm{xt}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\parallel},\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta}\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{xt}(\mathrm{HC}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta}\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}V^{*})=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{xt}(\mathcal{O},\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B}).

∎

7.
Computations

In this section we apply our methods to the homology of the closure of a sufficiently positive JM braid.
Recall that the variety
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{st}}
has a collection of the line bundles

\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
which are the structure sheaves of
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{st}}
with twisted
B^{2}
-equivariant
structure. More precisely, if
\chi_{i}
is the character of
B
that evaluates the
i
-th diagonal element, then

\mathcal{L}_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{st}}}\langle\chi_{i},0\rangle
where the characters in the brackets are the twisting characters of

B^{2}
. Denote

\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{L}^{a_{i}}_{i}.

We showed in
[
OR18b
]
that

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}=\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{st}}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}
, hence
our main theorem implies that the homology of the closure of
\delta^{\vec{a}}

is computed
in terms of hypercohomology of
\mathrm{CH}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{C}_{\parallel}^{\mathrm{st}}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}})
. Below we prove some vanishing result that allows to use
localization to compute this hypercohomology by localization method.

7.1.
Very positive JM braids

In general the object
\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
is an element of the category
\mathrm{D}^{per}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t)
.
There is no
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-localization theory for objects of the last category. However, there is a natural folding functor:

\mathrm{Fold}:\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t^{0})\to\mathrm{D}^{per}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t)

which is a composition of the shift functor

\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t^{0})\to\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t),(M_{i},d)\to(t^{i}\cdot M_{i},d)

and the functor that reduced the integral homological grading to two-periodic grading.

It is an interesting question for which
\beta\in\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
the element
\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})
is from the smaller category

\mathrm{Fold}(\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t^{0}))
. For example, if
\beta
is a half-twist on three strands, the computations from

[
OR18c
]
suggests that
\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})\notin\mathrm{Fold}(\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},q^{0}t^{0}))
. However, we propose the following

Conjecture 7.1.1
.

If
\beta
is such that
L(\beta)
is a link of planar singularity then

(1)

\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\beta})
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{Fold}(\mathcal{F}_{\beta})
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
is a
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-equivariant sheaf (a complex with a support in a single homological degree).

(2)

H^{>0}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{\beta})=0
.

Some support to the conjecture is provided in the paper
[
GKO22
]
. Another statement in the direction of the conjecture is the following

Theorem 7.1.2
.

Suppose that
\vec{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
such that
a_{i}\geq a_{j}-1
,
i>j
. Then there is a
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-equivariant sheaf

\mathcal{F}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}\in\textup{Coh}(\mathrm{Hilb}_{n},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})
such that

\mathbb{H}(\mathrm{CH}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}+c\mathbf{1}}})\otimes\Lambda\mathcal{B})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}),

if
c
is sufficiently positive.

Our proof relies on a construction of Chern functor for dualizable link homology
[
OR19
]
,
that is discussed in the next section, and Broer’s vanishing result
[
Bro93
]
.
In forthcoming work we prove analogous result for the braids that are composition of Coxeter braids
[
OR17
]
and JM braids.

7.2.
Another Chern functor

To prove theorem
7.1.2
we need to develop a version of the Chern functor for the dualizable homology from
[
OR19
]
.
In this note we only treat the case of dualizable homology of closure of pure braids. An extension to general braids presents no technical difficulties and will be done
in a subsequent publication. Let us recall some details of
[
OR19
]
.

Let us introduce a subspace
(G\times\mathfrak{b})\times_{\mathfrak{h}}(G\times\mathfrak{b})\subset(G\times\mathfrak{b})^{2}
that consists of
(g_{1},Y_{1},g_{2},Y_{2})
such that
the diagonal parts of
Y_{i}
are equal.
As explained in
[
OR19
]
, the group of pure braids
\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{Br}_{n}
can be realized inside monoidal category:

\underline{\Phi}:\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}\to(\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\textup{st}},\star),

\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\textup{st}}:=\mathrm{MF}((\mathfrak{g}\times(G\times\mathfrak{b})\times_{\mathfrak{h}}(G\times\mathfrak{b})\times V)^{\textup{st}},G\times B^{2}\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t,W),

W(X,g_{1},Y_{1},g_{2},Y_{2},v)=\textup{Tr}(X(\textup{Ad}_{g_{1}}Y_{1}-\textup{Ad}_{g_{2}}Y_{2}))

where
G\times B^{2}\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
is consistent with the action on
(\mathfrak{g}\times(G\times\mathfrak{b})^{2}\times V)^{\textup{st}}
and the convolution is defined by the
formula (
4.15
).

Similarly to previously discussed case we define map
j_{e,G}
by the formula
(
6.1
) and the functor
\mathbb{L}
:

\mathbb{L}\colon\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\mathrm{st}}\to\mathrm{MF}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}},B\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t,0),\quad\mathcal{C}\mapsto j^{*}_{e,G}(\mathcal{C})^{G},

where
\mathrm{MF}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t,0)
is the category of coherent sheaves on the quotient
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}/B
, where

\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}=\{(X,Y,v)\in\mathfrak{b}\times\mathfrak{b}\times V_{B}\;|\;\mathbb{C}\langle X,Y\rangle\,v=V_{B}\}.

The space
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}
has a natural involution
\mathfrak{F}
that switches two copies of
\mathfrak{b}
and it is shown in
[
OR19
]
that for any
\beta\in\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}
we have an isomorphism

(7.1)

\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi}(\beta))=\mathfrak{F}^{*}(\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi}(\beta))).

It is also shown
[
OR19
]
that the triply-graded space below is the link invariant of the closure
L(\beta)

\mathrm{HXY}^{\bullet}(\beta):=\mathrm{CE}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi}(\beta))\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}V)^{T}.

The LHS of the last formula is element of category
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathbb{C}_{x}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}_{y},q^{0}t)
just as LHS of (
6.2
) is element of
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{per}}(\mathbb{C}_{x}^{n},q^{0}t)
.
It is shown in
[
OR19
]
that there is a relation these two types of homology

\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\beta)=\mathrm{HXY}^{\bullet}(\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\otimes}}_{\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]}\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}],

where
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}]
and

\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}]=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
.

In this section we construct another version of the Chern functor:

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}:\underline{\mathrm{MF}}^{\mathrm{st}}\to\mathrm{Coh}(\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\times V,G\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t),

that allows us to compute our link homology. To explain the construction we need to introduce few auxiliary spaces.

Let us define a
G\times B\times B
-equivariant space

\underline{\mathscr{Y}}=\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}\times G\times G

with
G\times B^{2}
-action given by:

(h,b_{1},b_{2})\cdot(X,Y,v,k,g)=(\textup{Ad}_{b_{1}}X,\textup{Ad}_{b_{2}}Y,b_{1}v,b_{2}kb_{1}^{-1},hgb_{2}^{-1}).

To define
\underline{\mathrm{CH}}
we need two maps:

p:\mathscr{Y}\to\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}},\quad(X,Y,v,k,g)\mapsto(X,Y,v),

\mu^{\oplus 2}:\mathscr{Y}\to\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V,\quad(X,Y,v,k,g)\mapsto(\textup{Ad}_{gk}X,\textup{Ad}_{g}Y,gkv)

Finally, we define a two periodic complex
\mathrm{K}_{\Delta}=\mathrm{K}(k\in B)

on
\underline{\mathscr{Y}}
. Then we have for
\beta\in\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}(\beta)=\mu^{\oplus 2}_{*}(p^{*}(\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi}(\beta))\otimes\mathrm{K}_{\Delta}))^{B^{2}}.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

For any
\beta\in\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}
we have

\mathbb{H}((\underline{\mathrm{CH}}(\beta)\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B})^{G})=\mathrm{HXY}^{\bullet}(\beta)

Proof.

Indeed,
\mathbb{H}((\underline{\mathrm{CH}}(\beta)\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})^{G})=\mathbb{H}(p^{*}(\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi}(\beta))\otimes\mathrm{K}_{\Delta}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})^{G\times B^{2}})
. Let us use notation
B^{(i)}
for the
i
-th factor in
B^{2}
.
Since the action of
G\times B^{(2)}
on
\underline{\mathscr{Y}}
is free,

(p^{*}(\mathcal{S})\otimes\mathrm{K}_{\Delta}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})^{G\times B^{(2)}}
is homotopy equivalent to matrix factorization
\mathcal{S}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B}

on
\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}}
. Hence
\mathbb{H}((\mathcal{S}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})^{B})=\mathbb{H}(p^{*}(\mathcal{S})\otimes\mathrm{K}_{\Delta}\otimes\Lambda^{i}t\mathcal{B})^{G\times B^{2}})
and the statement follows.
∎

The
\textup{GL}_{n}
-cover
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}
of
\mathrm{Hilb}
is a subvariety of
\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V
, that is we have an embedding
i_{\mathrm{Hilb}}:\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}\to\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V
. Respectively,
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{y=0}
is a subvariety
defined by the nilpotency of
Y
in
(X,Y,v)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}
.
The subvariety
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{y=0}
is a complete intersection and let us denote
by
\mathrm{K}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{y=0})
the corresponding Koszul complex.

Conjecture 7.2.2
.

For any
\beta\in\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}
we have

i_{\mathrm{Hilb}*}(\mathrm{CH}^{\textup{st}}_{\textup{loc}}(\beta))=\underline{\mathrm{CH}}(\beta)\otimes\mathrm{K}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}_{y=0})

If the complex
\mathbb{L}(\underline{\Phi(\beta)})=\mathcal{S}_{\beta}
is obtained by folding from a bounded complex
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}}^{\mathrm{free}},B\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})
,

\mathcal{S}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Fold}(\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta})
then we call such braid

unfoldable
. If
\beta
is unfoldable we can define an unfolded
version of the Chern functor as

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\beta)=\mu^{\oplus 2}(p^{*}(\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}\otimes\hat{K}_{\Delta})),

where
\hat{K}_{\Delta}\in D^{b}(\underline{\mathscr{Y}},G\times B^{2}\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})
is
the Koszul complex of
k\in B
. The following statement follows immediately from the
above definitions:

Proposition 7.2.3
.

For an unfoldable
\beta\in\mathfrak{PBr}_{n}
such that
\mathbb{H}^{>0}(\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\beta)^{G})=0
we have:

\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}}((\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\beta)\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})^{G})=\dim_{q,t}(\mathrm{HXY}^{i}(\beta)).

In particular, JM braids
\beta=\delta^{\vec{a}}
are unfoldable since

\mathcal{S}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Fold}(\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}),\quad\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}=\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}

where
Z_{++}
is the strictly upper-triangular part of a matrix
Z
and

\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\widetilde{\mathrm{FHilb}},B\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})

is the Koszul complex for the subvariety defined by
[X,Y]=0
.

The subspace
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V
is a closed smooth subvariety of codimension
n^{2}-n
.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}}\in D^{b}(\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V,G\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})
the structure sheaf of the corresponding manifold.

Proposition 7.2.4
.

Let
\beta=\delta^{\vec{a}}
then

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\beta)=\mathrm{K}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}})\otimes\mu^{\oplus 2}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}})

Proof.

It is enough to show the statement for the case
\vec{a}=0
. The space
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}
is smooth hence there is a
G\times\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-invariant
cover
\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
such that
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}\cap U_{i}
is defined by

n^{2}-n
equations
E_{i}^{j}
,
j=1,\dots,n^{2}-n
. Let
\hat{K}(E_{i})
be the corresponding Koszul complex.

The preimage of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}
under
\mu^{\oplus 2}
is the flag Hilbert scheme

\mathrm{FHilb}
that is defined by the
n^{2}-n
equations:
k\in\mathfrak{b}
,
[X,Y]_{++}=0
,

X,Y\in\mathfrak{b}
. Hence there is isomorphism
f_{i}:p^{*}(\hat{\mathrm{K}}([X,Y]_{++}))\otimes\hat{K}_{\Delta}\to\mu^{\oplus 2*}(\hat{K}(E_{i}^{\bullet}))
.

On the intersection
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
there is an isomorphism

h_{ji}:\hat{K}(E_{i}^{\bullet})\to\hat{K}(E_{j}^{\bullet})
such that
g_{ij}=\mu^{\oplus 2*}(h_{ji})
is strictly upper-triangular.
Thus
\{g_{ij}\}
form a
G\times B^{2}
-equivariant cocycle with values in the unipotent group.

On the other hand
\underline{\mathscr{Y}}/B^{2}
retracts to
\mathrm{Fl}\times\mathrm{Fl}
thus has no odd cohomology. Hence the cocycle
\{g_{ij}\}
is a coboundary: there are

\phi_{i}
such that
\phi_{i}\phi_{j}^{-1}=f_{i}^{-1}g_{ij}f_{i}
.
Thus
\{f_{i}\phi_{i}\}
define desires isomorphism between
\mu^{\oplus 2*}(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}})
and
p^{*}(\hat{\mathrm{K}}([X,Y]_{++}))\otimes\hat{K}_{\Delta}
.
∎

Proposition 7.2.5
.

If
\vec{b}
satisfies conditions
a_{i}\geq a_{j}-1
for any
i,j
such that

i>j
. Then

(1)

\mathbb{H}^{>0}(\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\delta^{\vec{a}}))=0

(2)

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\delta^{\vec{a}})^{G}
is a free module over

\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}]

Proof.

First we observe that
\underline{\mathscr{Y}}/B^{2}
is a open piece of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\times\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\times V
. The map
\mu^{\oplus 2}
is equal to two copies of
the moment map
\mu^{\oplus 2}=\mu\times\mu\times 1
.

The open condition defining

\underline{\mathscr{Y}}/B^{2}
is pulled back from
\mathfrak{g}^{2}\times V
along
\mu^{\oplus 2}
.
Thus it is to prove the statement that the sheaf

\mathcal{G}_{\vec{a}}=(\mu\times\mu)_{*}(\mathcal{O}\boxtimes\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}})

has no higher cohomology and free over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}]^{G}
where
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
is the first copy
\mathfrak{g}^{2}
.

The second fact is standard: there is a
G
-equivariant natural projection from
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathfrak{h}
and the
G
-invariant functions on the fibers are constant,
see

[
CG97
]
. The vanishing result is a modification of the result from

[
Bro93
]
.
Indeed, it is shown in the cited paper that

(7.2)

H^{>0}(\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}}|_{T^{*}\mathrm{Fl}})=0

if the conditions of the statement hold.
On the other hand
\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}}|_{\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathrm{Fl}}=\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}}\otimes\mu^{*}(\mathrm{K}(\mathcal{N}))
where
\mathrm{K}(\mathcal{N})
the Koszul complex of
the nilpotent cone. Thus
H^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}}|_{\mathrm{T}^{*}\mathrm{Fl}})
is computed by a spectral sequence
E_{1}
terms
H^{j}(\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}})\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathfrak{h}
. Thus
we must have
H^{>0}(\mathcal{L}_{\vec{a}})=0
to ensure (
7.2
).
∎

7.3.
Proof of theorem
7.1.2

Proposition
7.2.4
and proposition
7.2.4
there is a sheaf

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}
such that

\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\delta_{\vec{a}})=j_{\widetilde{\mathrm{Hilb}}*}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}})
.
Let us denote by
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}
be the corresponding sheaf on

\mathrm{Hilb}
.

Since
\det(\mathcal{B})
is an ample bundle on
\mathrm{Hilb}
for sufficiently positive

c
the sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}
has no higher cohomology all
i
. Hence for
\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}}
the conditions of proposition
7.2.3
are satisfied and we have

H^{0}(\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}}}\otimes\Lambda^{i}(\mathcal{B}))=\mathrm{HXY}^{i}(\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}}).

Finally, we observe by proposition
7.2.5
the
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]
-module

\mathrm{HXY}^{\bullet}(\delta^{\vec{a}+c\mathbf{1}})
is a free over
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{x}]
.
Hence by the symmetry property it is a free module over
\mathbb{C}[\mathbf{y}]
. Thus we have

(7.3)

\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}(\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}})=\mathrm{HXY}^{\bullet}(\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}})|_{y=0}=\mathrm{H}^{0}(\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Hilb}_{y=0}}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}\otimes\Lambda^{\bullet}\mathcal{B}),

where
\mathrm{Hilb}_{y=0}
is defined by vanishing of
y\in H^{0}(\mathcal{B})
. Thus the theorem follows with
\mathcal{F}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}=\underline{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta^{\vec{a}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Hilb}_{y=0}}
.

7.4.
Proof of theorem
1.0.3

Corollary 7.4.1
.

Suppose
\vec{a}
and
c
satisfy conditions of theorem
7.1.2
then

\dim_{q,t}\mathrm{H}^{i}(\delta^{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}})=\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}}(\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})

where

\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\in D^{b}(\mathrm{FHilb}_{y=0}^{\mathrm{free}},\mathbb{T}_{q,t},q^{0}t^{0})

is the
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-equivariant Koszul complex of relations

[X,Y]_{i,j}=0
,
j-i>0
.

Proof.

Indeed by the equation (
7.3
) it is enough to show that

\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}}(\underline{\mathrm{CH}}^{b}(\delta_{\vec{a}})\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B})=\chi_{\mathbb{T}_{q,t}}(\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{B}).

The last equality is derived by the same argument as proposition
7.2.1
since

\mathrm{Fold}(\hat{K}([X,Y]_{++})\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c})=\mathbb{L}(\Phi(\delta_{\vec{a}+c\vec{1}})).

∎

To compute the Euler characteristics of a
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-equivariant complex on
\mathrm{FHilb}_{y=0}^{\mathrm{free}}
one can apply localization formula.
A description of the
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-fixed locus in
\mathrm{FHilb}_{y=0}^{\mathrm{free}}
is given
[
OR17
]
.
In the same paper the localization formula for the
\mathbb{T}_{q,t}
-equivariant Euler characteristics of
\hat{K}([X,Y])\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\vec{a}}\otimes\det(\mathcal{B})^{c}
is derived. The formula in
[
OR17
]
matches the formula in the statement of theorem
1.0.3
.
